# Unit 17 linguistic and owned-edition mapping packet

This packet prepares review. It records an unsigned structural mapping proposal. It does not record a qualified review, an approved edition alignment, or an install decision.

The owned-edition mapping worksheet separates observed edition facts, the proposed lesson and printed page range, unresolved edition questions, and matters that still require qualified linguistic review. ISBN, owned-copy verification, variance, reviewer identity, qualification, date, and approval stay blank. The disposition remains PENDING_CURRICULUM_REVIEWER.

Unit 17 remains in development. Its readiness is content-complete and review-pending. The Lesson 17 relationship is a proposed, unsigned mapping. It is not an approved edition alignment.

kind=TEACH
title=Ordinals agree like adjectives
body=Ordinals agree with their noun in gender, number and case, like adjectives. восемнадцатую девушку means the eighteenth girl as a feminine accusative noun phrase. The retained example comes from a sentence about photographing her; this displayed fragment alone does not mean She just photographed. The ordinal ending follows the noun phrase, rather than staying in the masculine dictionary form. These recognition checks do not assess producing agreement in a sentence.

### STEP `u17-st-rem-ordinals`

- Objectives: obj-u17-ordinals

- Content hash: cbff916516e144db8d8b8ab10efb03ec61b821fd071cfc8cc8c294b0066f0c48

kind=REMEDIATE
title=Review positions and counts
body=пе́рвый — first. второ́й — second. тре́тий — third. четвёртый — fourth. An ordinal identifies position in an ordered sequence; a cardinal tells how many. These isolated ordinal forms are masculine nominative singular. Learn the position expressed by each form before choosing it.

Ordinals agree with their noun in gender, number and case, like adjectives. восемнадцатую девушку means the eighteenth girl as a feminine accusative noun phrase. The retained example comes from a sentence about photographing her; this displayed fragment alone does not mean She just photographed. The ordinal ending follows the noun phrase, rather than staying in the masculine dictionary form. These recognition checks do not assess producing agreement in a sentence.

For the first ordinal, choose пе́рвый; for the second ordinal, choose второ́й; for the third ordinal, choose тре́тий. четвёртый expresses fourth, so it fails each of those requests. Read the English request: the separately shown Russian cue may name a different position and is not the target to copy.
dominantAction=Continue
references=

kind=REMEDIATE
title=Review positions and counts
body=один час — one hour. два часа — two hours. пять часов — five hours. The noun changes with the count: nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five. English uses hours for both two and five, but Russian uses different forms.

For basic counts in nominative or inanimate accusative constructions, use the one pattern for numbers ending in 1, the two pattern for endings 2, 3 or 4, and the five pattern for endings 0, 5, 6, 7, 8 or 9. Numbers ending in 11, 12, 13 or 14 take the five pattern instead. Other case constructions require additional changes to the numeral and noun; this is not a rule for every sentence position.

For a two-hour row, choose два часа. один час names one hour and пять часов names five hours. For a five-hour row, choose пять часов, even when the separately shown cue is the one-hour phrase. Read the requested quantity rather than copying the cue. Recognizing these hour counts does not demonstrate telling the time on a clock.

kind=TEACH
title=One, two and five years
body=один год — one year. два года — two years. пять лет — five years. The basic counting forms are nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five. The plural counting form for years uses a different stem: learn пять лет as the complete five-year phrase.

- Content hash: 0b2773328f8daf6b393d8cd3e3e7cc1d8eb326f32622d4f96adcf632a4a71de5

kind=TEACH
title=Compound counts and the teen exception
body=сорок один год — forty-one years. The compound ends in 1, so its noun follows the one pattern. In basic nominative or inanimate accusative counts, endings 2, 3 or 4 follow the two pattern; endings 0 and 5 through 9 follow the five pattern. Endings 11 through 14 are exceptions to the final-digit rule and take the five pattern. Other grammatical cases need additional changes.
dominantAction=Continue
references=ref-u17-attribution
items=

title=Work a year choice
body=For a five-year row, choose пять лет, rather than два года, which names two years. For a one-year row, choose один год; for a two-year row, choose два года. The separately shown cue can have a different count. These checks recognize quantities of years, not ordinal calendar-year dates or a complete age sentence.

kind=TEACH
title=Admitted instruction
body=In Russia, dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "28.08.17") and the expanded form (for example "28 августа 2017 г."); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (goda, "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "28.08.2017") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral grammatical gender), then the month in genitive case (for example "Двадцать восьмое августа").
dominantAction=Continue
references=ref-u17-attribution
items=
examples=
unavailableReason=
bookDirection=
companionDirection=
remediationStepId=

title=Unit 17 source notice
summary=Proposed Unit 17 adapts CC BY-SA 4.0 dictionary rows, Wikibooks excerpts, and the admitted Wikipedia clock and date spans.
detail=OpenRussian dictionary data is frozen at commit 50e210c4803237779cb562bc1abcea529066031c under CC BY-SA 4.0. Wikibooks excerpts are frozen by oldid under the CC BY-SA 4.0 route recorded on Wikibooks:Copyrights oldid 4622060. The bounded clock phrases are frozen at Wikipedia oldid 1366690592. The calendar-date examples are frozen at Wikipedia oldid 1357134172. Those license routes are not interchangeable. The admitted calendar-date relationships remain admitted. The three clock phrases are bounded recognition. Quarter-hour lines use a separate Pressbooks notice and do not clear productive clock-time. Productive clock-time is the ReadyRussian Grammar 20 system applied to exact retained expressions for minutes into the hour, minutes remaining after the half hour, and dial reading. The governed span is a text extraction. The clock image, data URI, quarter-hour lines, and linked audio are not admitted. Review is pending.

мне двадцать лет
I am 20 years old
Which natural English states the age in the shown phrase?
мне двадцать лет means I am 20 years old. A labeled literal gloss is a different question.
The age construction мне двадцать лет is natural English I am 20 years old.
I own 20 summers
20 people asked me
I own 20 summers invents possession that the age construction does not state.
20 people asked me invents a different predicate.

г.
year, the abbreviation
What does the abbreviation г. contribute in a date?
г. abbreviates год, year. The rest of a printed date is a separate question.
The abbreviation г. means year.
the month name
the day number
the month name is a separate word in the date, not the abbreviation г.
the day number is a separate part of the date, not the abbreviation г.

### PROVENANCE `prov-ca-u17-numbers-hours-rem-body`

- Objectives: obj-u17-hours
- Sessions: u17-s-orient, u17-s-hours
- Source: src-content-audit-research
- Rights: rights-content-audit-research
- Classification: none
- Current review status: IMPLEMENTED_PENDING_FINAL_AUDIT
- Content hash: d507a50300fcf3d82ec11d8e197fb040e140286fa2c59fb8a35a8d438705e741

recordType=label
sourceUrl=urn:russian-companion:content-audit-research:2026-10-08
sourceRevision=content-audit-2026-10-08
sourceRecordId=ca-coverage:obj-u17-hours:numbers:hours-rem-body
originalSourceText=Retained Wikibooks Russian/Grammar/Numbers oldid 4371358, catalog/sources/units-6-10/wikibooks/numbers-4371358.txt: ordinal adjective agreement, numeral gender, count noun cases, compound counts and teen exceptions. Primary learning grammar https://russky.info/grammar/numerals?hl=en, consulted 2026-10-11 UTC: ordinal positions and adjective agreement; counting constructions. Application inference: explain existing Unit 17 forms and the requested positions or quantities; distinguish the retained ordinal noun phrase from the full source sentence gloss. Independently authored English 2026-10-11 UTC; final linguistic audit pending.
один час — one hour. два часа — two hours. пять часов — five hours. The noun changes with the count: nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five. English uses hours for both two and five, but Russian uses different forms.

For basic counts in nominative or inanimate accusative constructions, use the one pattern for numbers ending in 1, the two pattern for endings 2, 3 or 4, and the five pattern for endings 0, 5, 6, 7, 8 or 9. Numbers ending in 11, 12, 13 or 14 take the five pattern instead. Other case constructions require additional changes to the numeral and noun; this is not a rule for every sentence position.

For a two-hour row, choose два часа. один час names one hour and пять часов names five hours. For a five-hour row, choose пять часов, even when the separately shown cue is the one-hour phrase. Read the requested quantity rather than copying the cue. Recognizing these hour counts does not demonstrate telling the time on a clock.
shippedText=один час — one hour. два часа — two hours. пять часов — five hours. The noun changes with the count: nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five. English uses hours for both two and five, but Russian uses different forms.

For basic counts in nominative or inanimate accusative constructions, use the one pattern for numbers ending in 1, the two pattern for endings 2, 3 or 4, and the five pattern for endings 0, 5, 6, 7, 8 or 9. Numbers ending in 11, 12, 13 or 14 take the five pattern instead. Other case constructions require additional changes to the numeral and noun; this is not a rule for every sentence position.

For a two-hour row, choose два часа. один час names one hour and пять часов names five hours. For a five-hour row, choose пять часов, even when the separately shown cue is the one-hour phrase. Read the requested quantity rather than copying the cue. Recognizing these hour counts does not demonstrate telling the time on a clock.

Numbers ending in 11, 12, 13 or 14 take the five pattern instead. Other case constructions require additional changes to the numeral and noun; this is not a rule for every sentence position.

recordType=label
sourceUrl=urn:russian-companion:content-audit-research:2026-10-08
sourceRevision=content-audit-2026-10-08
sourceRecordId=ca-coverage:obj-u17-minutes:numbers:minutes-rem-body
originalSourceText=Retained Wikibooks Russian/Grammar/Numbers oldid 4371358, catalog/sources/units-6-10/wikibooks/numbers-4371358.txt: ordinal adjective agreement, numeral gender, count noun cases, compound counts and teen exceptions. Primary learning grammar https://russky.info/grammar/numerals?hl=en, consulted 2026-10-11 UTC: ordinal positions and adjective agreement; counting constructions. Application inference: explain existing Unit 17 forms and the requested positions or quantities; distinguish the retained ordinal noun phrase from the full source sentence gloss. Independently authored English 2026-10-11 UTC; final linguistic audit pending.
одна минута — one minute. две минуты — two minutes. пять минут — five minutes. The noun for minute is feminine, so one and two use feminine forms here. Compare the masculine hour and year phrases. The noun is nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five.

For basic nominative or inanimate accusative counts, an ending of 1 follows the one-minute pattern, endings 2, 3 or 4 follow the two-minute pattern, and endings 0 or 5 through 9 follow the five-minute pattern. Endings 11 through 14 take the five-minute pattern instead of following the final digit alone. Different case constructions require additional numeral and noun changes.

For a one-minute row, choose одна минута. For a two-minute row, choose две минуты. For a five-minute row, choose пять минут. Each other phrase names a different quantity. Read the English request even if the separately shown cue has a different count. These quantity choices do not assess expressing minutes past an hour on a clock.
shippedText=одна минута — one minute. две минуты — two minutes. пять минут — five minutes. The noun for minute is feminine, so one and two use feminine forms here. Compare the masculine hour and year phrases. The noun is nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five.

For basic nominative or inanimate accusative counts, an ending of 1 follows the one-minute pattern, endings 2, 3 or 4 follow the two-minute pattern, and endings 0 or 5 through 9 follow the five-minute pattern. Endings 11 through 14 take the five-minute pattern instead of following the final digit alone. Different case constructions require additional numeral and noun changes.

For a one-minute row, choose одна минута. For a two-minute row, choose две минуты. For a five-minute row, choose пять минут. Each other phrase names a different quantity. Read the English request even if the separately shown cue has a different count. These quantity choices do not assess expressing minutes past an hour on a clock.

Endings 11 through 14 take the five-minute pattern instead of following the final digit alone. Different case constructions require additional numeral and noun changes.

- Content hash: 8f911b2108ec8f25e88080bb9148703fdd368acc669c4aa680d97c45f10bd4e8

recordType=label
sourceUrl=urn:russian-companion:content-audit-research:2026-10-08
sourceRevision=content-audit-2026-10-08
sourceRecordId=ca-coverage:obj-u17-ordinals:numbers:ordinals-rem-body
originalSourceText=Retained Wikibooks Russian/Grammar/Numbers oldid 4371358, catalog/sources/units-6-10/wikibooks/numbers-4371358.txt: ordinal adjective agreement, numeral gender, count noun cases, compound counts and teen exceptions. Primary learning grammar https://russky.info/grammar/numerals?hl=en, consulted 2026-10-11 UTC: ordinal positions and adjective agreement; counting constructions. Application inference: explain existing Unit 17 forms and the requested positions or quantities; distinguish the retained ordinal noun phrase from the full source sentence gloss. Independently authored English 2026-10-11 UTC; final linguistic audit pending.
пе́рвый — first. второ́й — second. тре́тий — third. четвёртый — fourth. An ordinal identifies position in an ordered sequence; a cardinal tells how many. These isolated ordinal forms are masculine nominative singular. Learn the position expressed by each form before choosing it.

Ordinals agree with their noun in gender, number and case, like adjectives. восемнадцатую девушку means the eighteenth girl as a feminine accusative noun phrase. The retained example comes from a sentence about photographing her; this displayed fragment alone does not mean She just photographed. The ordinal ending follows the noun phrase, rather than staying in the masculine dictionary form. These recognition checks do not assess producing agreement in a sentence.

For the first ordinal, choose пе́рвый; for the second ordinal, choose второ́й; for the third ordinal, choose тре́тий. четвёртый expresses fourth, so it fails each of those requests. Read the English request: the separately shown Russian cue may name a different position and is not the target to copy.

Ordinals agree with their noun in gender, number and case, like adjectives. восемнадцатую девушку means the eighteenth girl as a feminine accusative noun phrase. The retained example comes from a sentence about photographing her; this displayed fragment alone does not mean She just photographed. The ordinal ending follows the noun phrase, rather than staying in the masculine dictionary form. These recognition checks do not assess producing agreement in a sentence.

For the first ordinal, choose пе́рвый; for the second ordinal, choose второ́й; for the third ordinal, choose тре́тий. четвёртый expresses fourth, so it fails each of those requests. Read the English request: the separately shown Russian cue may name a different position and is not the target to copy.

The retained example comes from a sentence about photographing her; this displayed fragment alone does not mean She just photographed. The ordinal ending follows the noun phrase, rather than staying in the masculine dictionary form. These recognition checks do not assess producing agreement in a sentence.

recordType=label
sourceUrl=urn:russian-companion:content-audit-research:2026-10-08
sourceRevision=content-audit-2026-10-08
sourceRecordId=ca-coverage:obj-u17-years:numbers:years-rem-body
originalSourceText=Retained Wikibooks Russian/Grammar/Numbers oldid 4371358, catalog/sources/units-6-10/wikibooks/numbers-4371358.txt: ordinal adjective agreement, numeral gender, count noun cases, compound counts and teen exceptions. Primary learning grammar https://russky.info/grammar/numerals?hl=en, consulted 2026-10-11 UTC: ordinal positions and adjective agreement; counting constructions. Application inference: explain existing Unit 17 forms and the requested positions or quantities; distinguish the retained ordinal noun phrase from the full source sentence gloss. Independently authored English 2026-10-11 UTC; final linguistic audit pending.
один год — one year. два года — two years. пять лет — five years. The basic counting forms are nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five. The plural counting form for years uses a different stem: learn пять лет as the complete five-year phrase.

сорок один год — forty-one years. The compound ends in 1, so its noun follows the one pattern. In basic nominative or inanimate accusative counts, endings 2, 3 or 4 follow the two pattern; endings 0 and 5 through 9 follow the five pattern. Endings 11 through 14 are exceptions to the final-digit rule and take the five pattern. Other grammatical cases need additional changes.

For a five-year row, choose пять лет, rather than два года, which names two years. For a one-year row, choose один год; for a two-year row, choose два года. The separately shown cue can have a different count. These checks recognize quantities of years, not ordinal calendar-year dates or a complete age sentence.
shippedText=один год — one year. два года — two years. пять лет — five years. The basic counting forms are nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five. The plural counting form for years uses a different stem: learn пять лет as the complete five-year phrase.

сорок один год — forty-one years. The compound ends in 1, so its noun follows the one pattern. In basic nominative or inanimate accusative counts, endings 2, 3 or 4 follow the two pattern; endings 0 and 5 through 9 follow the five pattern. Endings 11 through 14 are exceptions to the final-digit rule and take the five pattern. Other grammatical cases need additional changes.

For a five-year row, choose пять лет, rather than два года, which names two years. For a one-year row, choose один год; for a two-year row, choose два года. The separately shown cue can have a different count. These checks recognize quantities of years, not ordinal calendar-year dates or a complete age sentence.

originalSourceText=Retained Wikibooks Russian/Grammar/Numbers oldid 4371358, catalog/sources/units-6-10/wikibooks/numbers-4371358.txt: ordinal adjective agreement, numeral gender, count noun cases, compound counts and teen exceptions. Independently authored English 2026-10-11 UTC; final linguistic audit pending.
один год — one year. два года — two years. пять лет — five years. The basic counting forms are nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five. The plural counting form for years uses a different stem: learn пять лет as the complete five-year phrase.
shippedText=один год — one year. два года — two years. пять лет — five years. The basic counting forms are nominative singular after one, genitive singular after two, and genitive plural after five. The plural counting form for years uses a different stem: learn пять лет as the complete five-year phrase.

originalSourceText=Retained Wikibooks Russian/Grammar/Numbers oldid 4371358, catalog/sources/units-6-10/wikibooks/numbers-4371358.txt: ordinal adjective agreement, numeral gender, count noun cases, compound counts and teen exceptions. Primary learning grammar https://russky.info/grammar/numerals?hl=en, consulted 2026-10-11 UTC: ordinal positions and adjective agreement; counting constructions. Application inference: explain existing Unit 17 forms and the requested positions or quantities; distinguish the retained ordinal noun phrase from the full source sentence gloss. Independently authored English 2026-10-11 UTC; final linguistic audit pending.
сорок один год — forty-one years. The compound ends in 1, so its noun follows the one pattern. In basic nominative or inanimate accusative counts, endings 2, 3 or 4 follow the two pattern; endings 0 and 5 through 9 follow the five pattern. Endings 11 through 14 are exceptions to the final-digit rule and take the five pattern. Other grammatical cases need additional changes.
shippedText=сорок один год — forty-one years. The compound ends in 1, so its noun follows the one pattern. In basic nominative or inanimate accusative counts, endings 2, 3 or 4 follow the two pattern; endings 0 and 5 through 9 follow the five pattern. Endings 11 through 14 are exceptions to the final-digit rule and take the five pattern. Other grammatical cases need additional changes.

sourceRecordId=date-russia:In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This o
originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").
shippedText=the month in genitive case

originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Date_and_time_notation_in_Russia&oldid=1357134172
sourceRevision=1357134172
sourceRecordId=date-russia:In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This o
originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").
shippedText=day month year
transformation=selection

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://www.readyrussian.org/Handouts/Grammar%2020--Telling%20Time.html
sourceRevision=b6702e45884f1ab6ec0334c884393f23f1d61a7ed1fa3d764508ef1de9cd87ff
sourceRecordId=grammar20:We can read off the numbers shown on the dial. When we do so we are stating the 
originalSourceText=We can read off the numbers shown on the dial. When we do so we are stating the number of whole hours and minutes which have passed since noon or midnight. This option is always available.
In Russian we can state the number of minutes into the hour which is in progress. This makes sense up to the half hour.
We can say how many minutes remain until the little hand passes the next number on the dial. This is used after the half hour.
We can speak of quarters and halves of an hour rather than of minutes.
If the precise time is not important, then in Russian we can say things like “at the beginning of the hour” to indicate a time close to the start of the our or “at the end of the hour” for a time near its end.

The concept of the hour in progress will be unfamiliar to most English speakers. It is important to understand that “the first hour” does not mean one o'clock. It means the sixty-minute period which starts at 12 o'clock and ends at one o'clock. In the examples below the hour in progress is represented by a shaded pie slice.

The first hour began at 12 o'clock. To say that something happens at the time shown at left:
в двенадцать часов десять минут—at twelve hours ten minutes
в десять минут первого—at ten minutes of the first
в начале первого часа—at the beginning of the first hour

As the little hand passes the 1 the first hour ends and the second begins. To specify this time use any of these expressions:
в час пятнадцать минут—at [one] hour fifteen minutes
в пятнадцать минут второго—at the fifteenth minute of the second [hour]

в час двадцать пять минут—at [one] hour twenty five minutes
в двадцать пять минут второго—at twenty five minutes of the second [hour]

Once the hour is half-way done we may choose to say how soon the little hand will pass the next number. To specify the time shown at left:
в час тридцать пять минут—at [one] hour thirty five minutes
без двадцати пяти два—lacking twenty five minutes to the two
shippedText=at the fifteenth minute of the second [hour]
transformation=readyrussian text extraction
licenseId=CC BY-SA 4.0
licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/
attribution=ReadyRussian.org, CC BY-SA 4.0. Material marked CC BY-SA is licensed under the Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike 4.0 International License. {|

| '''Russian''' || '''English''' || '''Transcription'''

|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Год || Year || Goad
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Январь || January || Yanvár'
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Февраль || February || Fevrál'
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Март || March || Mart
|-
| &nbsp;
|-

|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Май || May || Mai
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Июнь || June || Iyún'
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Июль || July || Iyúl'
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Август || August || Avgust
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Сентябрь || September || Sentyábr'
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Октябрь || October || Oktyábr'
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Ноябрь || November || Noyábr'
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| Декабрь || December

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Numbers?oldid=4371358
sourceRevision=4371358
sourceRecordId=numbers:==Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal wit
originalSourceText===Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal with.  They all decline like adjectives.  For example: Только что фотографировала восемнадцатую девушку (She just photographed the eighteenth girl).

# п'''е'''рвый, п'''е'''рвая, п'''е'''рвое
# втор'''о'''й, втор'''а'''я, втор'''о'''е
# тр'''е'''тий, тр'''е'''тья, тр'''е'''тье
# четв'''ё'''ртый

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal with.  They all decline like adjectives.  For example: Только что фотографировала восемнадцатую девушку (She just photographed the eighteenth girl).

# п'''е'''рвый, п'''е'''рвая, п'''е'''рвое
# втор'''о'''й, втор'''а'''я, втор'''о'''е
# тр'''е'''тий, тр'''е'''тья, тр'''е'''тье
# четв'''ё'''ртый

### PROVENANCE `prov-ex-u17-quarter-hour-0-en`

- Objectives: obj-u17-quarter-hour
- Sessions: u17-s-orient, u17-s-quarter-hour
- Source: src-pressbooks-diverse-russian-traditions-grammar
- Rights: rights-pressbooks-diverse-russian-traditions-grammar
- Classification: none
- Current review status: IMPLEMENTED_PENDING_FINAL_AUDIT
- Content hash: 7d645f6626e3eecdd91268decd8ad3d8b60f4d5a24451518db7eb1c05d3f3d9e

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://wisc.pb.unizin.org/diverserussian/chapter/russia-traditions-grammar/
sourceRevision=ab2170e37d529a318f395f6e657d8024d0eaed8d7c5c9b34f8831d348b8b76b3
sourceRecordId=pressbooks-traditions-grammar:Грамматика: At what time on the quarter hour</strong></p>
</header>
<div class="
originalSourceText=Грамматика: At what time on the quarter hour</strong></p>
</header>
<div class="textbox__content">
<table class="grid" style="border-collapse: collapse;width: 100%">
<thead>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>Time</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%"><strong>Explanation and examples</strong></td>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the hour (0.00)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative, but note that the number then often requires genitive case after it. In informal speech, sometimes people leave out час / часа / часов. Remember: один час; два / три / четыре часа; пять часов.
<p>Концерт начинается <strong>в шесть часов</strong>. (18.00)<br>
Она была дома <strong>в час</strong>. (13.00)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the quarter hour (0.15)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative with either <strong>четверть</strong> (quarter) or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the ordinal number of the next hour in the genitive case. In essence you are saying quarter of the next hour. Note that while you can say 6:15 as “шесть пятнадцать”, in the exercises on this page we would like to focus on this other way of expressing the time.
<p>Я приду <strong>в четверть восьмого</strong>. (7.15 – quarter of the eighth hour)<br>
Он нам позвонил <strong>в пятнадцать минут десятого</strong>. (9.15 – 15 minutes of the tenth hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the half hour (0.30)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + prepositional with <strong>половина</strong> and the genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour, or colloquially, <strong>пол_____</strong> (genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour). Note that the spelling of 10:30 adds a hyphen: пол-одиннадцатого.
<p>Наш рейс уезжает <strong>в половине пятого</strong>. (4.30 – half of the fifth hour)<br>
Мы будем свободны <strong>в полвторого</strong>. (1.30 – half of the second hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the three quarters hour (0.45)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>без</strong> + genitive with <strong>четверть</strong> or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the cardinal number of the next hour in the nominative case. In essence you are saying X hours without X minutes.
<p>Передача начинается <strong>без четверти три</strong>. (2.45 – 3 without a quarter)<br>
Она обычно приходит на работу <strong>без пятнадцати девять</strong>. (8.45 – 9 without 15 minutes)</p>
shippedText=18.00
transformation=pressbooks markup selection
licenseId=CC BY-SA 4.0
licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/
attribution=Anna Tumarkin and Shannon Donnally Quinn, Diverse Russian: A Multicultural Exploration, CC BY-SA 4.0, except where otherwise noted. contentHash=3dbbd4fac86f79cb1cd81395763c63c440a5ab6cef42855f1d1d861a5dd83776

Note that the spelling of 10:30 adds a hyphen: пол-одиннадцатого.
<p>Наш рейс уезжает <strong>в половине пятого</strong>. (4.30 – half of the fifth hour)<br>

<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>

</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the three quarters hour (0.45)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>без</strong> + genitive with <strong>четверть</strong> or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the cardinal number of the next hour in the nominative case. In essence you are saying X hours without X minutes.

<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the quarter hour (0.15)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative with either <strong>четверть</strong> (quarter) or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the ordinal number of the next hour in the genitive case. In essence you are saying quarter of the next hour. Note that while you can say 6:15 as “шесть пятнадцать”, in the exercises on this page we would like to focus on this other way of expressing the time.
<p>Я приду <strong>в четверть восьмого</strong>. (7.15 – quarter of the eighth hour)<br>
Он нам позвонил <strong>в пятнадцать минут десятого</strong>. (9.15 – 15 minutes of the tenth hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the half hour (0.30)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + prepositional with <strong>половина</strong> and the genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour, or colloquially, <strong>пол_____</strong> (genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour). Note that the spelling of 10:30 adds a hyphen: пол-одиннадцатого.
<p>Наш рейс уезжает <strong>в половине пятого</strong>. (4.30 – half of the fifth hour)<br>
Мы будем свободны <strong>в полвторого</strong>. (1.30 – half of the second hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the three quarters hour (0.45)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>без</strong> + genitive with <strong>четверть</strong> or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the cardinal number of the next hour in the nominative case. In essence you are saying X hours without X minutes.
<p>Передача начинается <strong>без четверти три</strong>. (2.45 – 3 without a quarter)<br>
Она обычно приходит на работу <strong>без пятнадцати девять</strong>. (8.45 – 9 without 15 minutes)</p>

<p>Наш рейс уезжает <strong>в половине пятого</strong>. (4.30 – half of the fifth hour)<br>
Мы будем свободны <strong>в полвторого</strong>. (1.30 – half of the second hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the three quarters hour (0.45)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>без</strong> + genitive with <strong>четверть</strong> or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the cardinal number of the next hour in the nominative case. In essence you are saying X hours without X minutes.
<p>Передача начинается <strong>без четверти три</strong>. (2.45 – 3 without a quarter)<br>
Она обычно приходит на работу <strong>без пятнадцати девять</strong>. (8.45 – 9 without 15 minutes)</p>

<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>Time</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%"><strong>Explanation and examples</strong></td>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the hour (0.00)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative, but note that the number then often requires genitive case after it. In informal speech, sometimes people leave out час / часа / часов. Remember: один час; два / три / четыре часа; пять часов.

<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative with either <strong>четверть</strong> (quarter) or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. <td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + prepositional with <strong>половина</strong> and the genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour, or colloquially, <strong>пол_____</strong> (genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour). Note that the spelling of 10:30 adds a hyphen: пол-одиннадцатого.
<p>Наш рейс уезжает <strong>в половине пятого</strong>. (4.30 – half of the fifth hour)<br>

</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the three quarters hour (0.45)</strong></td>

- Objectives: obj-u17-quarter-hour
- Sessions: u17-s-orient, u17-s-quarter-hour
- Source: src-pressbooks-diverse-russian-traditions-grammar
- Rights: rights-pressbooks-diverse-russian-traditions-grammar
- Classification: none
- Current review status: IMPLEMENTED_PENDING_FINAL_AUDIT
- Content hash: 4313ae99219a7be6ef0279b40cf81d3c0a0c7404c4615ba6da70f2040fca05d5

recordType=example
sourceUrl=https://wisc.pb.unizin.org/diverserussian/chapter/russia-traditions-grammar/
sourceRevision=ab2170e37d529a318f395f6e657d8024d0eaed8d7c5c9b34f8831d348b8b76b3
sourceRecordId=pressbooks-traditions-grammar:Грамматика: At what time on the quarter hour</strong></p>
</header>
<div class="
originalSourceText=Грамматика: At what time on the quarter hour</strong></p>
</header>
<div class="textbox__content">
<table class="grid" style="border-collapse: collapse;width: 100%">
<thead>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>Time</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%"><strong>Explanation and examples</strong></td>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the hour (0.00)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative, but note that the number then often requires genitive case after it. In informal speech, sometimes people leave out час / часа / часов. Remember: один час; два / три / четыре часа; пять часов.
<p>Концерт начинается <strong>в шесть часов</strong>. (18.00)<br>
Она была дома <strong>в час</strong>. (13.00)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the quarter hour (0.15)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative with either <strong>четверть</strong> (quarter) or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the ordinal number of the next hour in the genitive case. In essence you are saying quarter of the next hour. Note that while you can say 6:15 as “шесть пятнадцать”, in the exercises on this page we would like to focus on this other way of expressing the time.
<p>Я приду <strong>в четверть восьмого</strong>. (7.15 – quarter of the eighth hour)<br>
Он нам позвонил <strong>в пятнадцать минут десятого</strong>. (9.15 – 15 minutes of the tenth hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the half hour (0.30)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + prepositional with <strong>половина</strong> and the genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour, or colloquially, <strong>пол_____</strong> (genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour). Note that the spelling of 10:30 adds a hyphen: пол-одиннадцатого.
<p>Наш рейс уезжает <strong>в половине пятого</strong>. (4.30 – half of the fifth hour)<br>
Мы будем свободны <strong>в полвторого</strong>. (1.30 – half of the second hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the three quarters hour (0.45)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>без</strong> + genitive with <strong>четверть</strong> or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the cardinal number of the next hour in the nominative case. In essence you are saying X hours without X minutes.
<p>Передача начинается <strong>без четверти три</strong>. (2.45 – 3 without a quarter)<br>
Она обычно приходит на работу <strong>без пятнадцати девять</strong>. (8.45 – 9 without 15 minutes)</p>
shippedText=Он нам позвонил в пятнадцать минут десятого.

licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/
attribution=Anna Tumarkin and Shannon Donnally Quinn, Diverse Russian: A Multicultural Exploration, CC BY-SA 4.0, except where otherwise noted. recordType=example
sourceUrl=https://wisc.pb.unizin.org/diverserussian/chapter/russia-traditions-grammar/
sourceRevision=ab2170e37d529a318f395f6e657d8024d0eaed8d7c5c9b34f8831d348b8b76b3
sourceRecordId=pressbooks-traditions-grammar:Грамматика: At what time on the quarter hour</strong></p>
</header>
<div class="
originalSourceText=Грамматика: At what time on the quarter hour</strong></p>
</header>
<div class="textbox__content">
<table class="grid" style="border-collapse: collapse;width: 100%">
<thead>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>Time</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%"><strong>Explanation and examples</strong></td>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the hour (0.00)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative, but note that the number then often requires genitive case after it. In informal speech, sometimes people leave out час / часа / часов. Remember: один час; два / три / четыре часа; пять часов.
<p>Концерт начинается <strong>в шесть часов</strong>. (18.00)<br>
Она была дома <strong>в час</strong>. (13.00)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the quarter hour (0.15)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative with either <strong>четверть</strong> (quarter) or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the ordinal number of the next hour in the genitive case. In essence you are saying quarter of the next hour. Note that while you can say 6:15 as “шесть пятнадцать”, in the exercises on this page we would like to focus on this other way of expressing the time.
<p>Я приду <strong>в четверть восьмого</strong>. (7.15 – quarter of the eighth hour)<br>
Он нам позвонил <strong>в пятнадцать минут десятого</strong>. (9.15 – 15 minutes of the tenth hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the half hour (0.30)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + prepositional with <strong>половина</strong> and the genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour, or colloquially, <strong>пол_____</strong> (genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour). Note that the spelling of 10:30 adds a hyphen: пол-одиннадцатого.
<p>Наш рейс уезжает <strong>в половине пятого</strong>. (4.30 – half of the fifth hour)<br>
Мы будем свободны <strong>в полвторого</strong>. (1.30 – half of the second hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the three quarters hour (0.45)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>без</strong> + genitive with <strong>четверть</strong> or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the cardinal number of the next hour in the nominative case. In essence you are saying X hours without X minutes.
<p>Передача начинается <strong>без четверти три</strong>. (2.45 – 3 without a quarter)<br>
Она обычно приходит на работу <strong>без пятнадцати девять</strong>. (8.45 – 9 without 15 minutes)</p>
shippedText=Она обычно приходит на работу без пятнадцати девять.
transformation=pressbooks markup selection

licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Date_and_time_notation_in_Russia&oldid=1357134172
sourceRevision=1357134172
sourceRecordId=date-russia:In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This o
originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").
shippedText=In Russia, dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "28.08.17") and the expanded form (for example "28 августа 2017 г."); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (goda, "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "28.08.2017") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral grammatical gender), then the month in genitive case (for example "Двадцать восьмое августа").
transformation=wiki label selection

Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").
shippedText=is short for

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://www.readyrussian.org/Handouts/Grammar%2020--Telling%20Time.html
sourceRevision=b6702e45884f1ab6ec0334c884393f23f1d61a7ed1fa3d764508ef1de9cd87ff
sourceRecordId=grammar20:We can read off the numbers shown on the dial. When we do so we are stating the 
originalSourceText=We can read off the numbers shown on the dial. When we do so we are stating the number of whole hours and minutes which have passed since noon or midnight. This option is always available.
In Russian we can state the number of minutes into the hour which is in progress. This makes sense up to the half hour.
We can say how many minutes remain until the little hand passes the next number on the dial. This is used after the half hour.
We can speak of quarters and halves of an hour rather than of minutes.
If the precise time is not important, then in Russian we can say things like “at the beginning of the hour” to indicate a time close to the start of the our or “at the end of the hour” for a time near its end.

The concept of the hour in progress will be unfamiliar to most English speakers. It is important to understand that “the first hour” does not mean one o'clock. It means the sixty-minute period which starts at 12 o'clock and ends at one o'clock. In the examples below the hour in progress is represented by a shaded pie slice.

The first hour began at 12 o'clock. To say that something happens at the time shown at left:
в двенадцать часов десять минут—at twelve hours ten minutes
в десять минут первого—at ten minutes of the first
в начале первого часа—at the beginning of the first hour

As the little hand passes the 1 the first hour ends and the second begins. To specify this time use any of these expressions:
в час пятнадцать минут—at [one] hour fifteen minutes
в пятнадцать минут второго—at the fifteenth minute of the second [hour]

в час двадцать пять минут—at [one] hour twenty five minutes
в двадцать пять минут второго—at twenty five minutes of the second [hour]

Once the hour is half-way done we may choose to say how soon the little hand will pass the next number. To specify the time shown at left:
в час тридцать пять минут—at [one] hour thirty five minutes
без двадцати пяти два—lacking twenty five minutes to the two
shippedText=In Russian we can state the number of minutes into the hour which is in progress.
transformation=readyrussian text extraction

sourceUrl=https://www.readyrussian.org/Handouts/Grammar%2020--Telling%20Time.html
sourceRevision=b6702e45884f1ab6ec0334c884393f23f1d61a7ed1fa3d764508ef1de9cd87ff
sourceRecordId=grammar20:We can read off the numbers shown on the dial. When we do so we are stating the 
originalSourceText=We can read off the numbers shown on the dial. When we do so we are stating the number of whole hours and minutes which have passed since noon or midnight. This option is always available.
In Russian we can state the number of minutes into the hour which is in progress. This makes sense up to the half hour.
We can say how many minutes remain until the little hand passes the next number on the dial. This is used after the half hour.
We can speak of quarters and halves of an hour rather than of minutes.
If the precise time is not important, then in Russian we can say things like “at the beginning of the hour” to indicate a time close to the start of the our or “at the end of the hour” for a time near its end.

The concept of the hour in progress will be unfamiliar to most English speakers. It is important to understand that “the first hour” does not mean one o'clock. It means the sixty-minute period which starts at 12 o'clock and ends at one o'clock. In the examples below the hour in progress is represented by a shaded pie slice.

The first hour began at 12 o'clock. To say that something happens at the time shown at left:
в двенадцать часов десять минут—at twelve hours ten minutes
в десять минут первого—at ten minutes of the first
в начале первого часа—at the beginning of the first hour

As the little hand passes the 1 the first hour ends and the second begins. To specify this time use any of these expressions:
в час пятнадцать минут—at [one] hour fifteen minutes
в пятнадцать минут второго—at the fifteenth minute of the second [hour]

в час двадцать пять минут—at [one] hour twenty five minutes
в двадцать пять минут второго—at twenty five minutes of the second [hour]

Once the hour is half-way done we may choose to say how soon the little hand will pass the next number. To specify the time shown at left:
в час тридцать пять минут—at [one] hour thirty five minutes
без двадцати пяти два—lacking twenty five minutes to the two
shippedText=This is used after the half hour.
transformation=readyrussian text extraction

recordType=label
sourceUrl=https://www.readyrussian.org/Handouts/Grammar%2020--Telling%20Time.html
sourceRevision=b6702e45884f1ab6ec0334c884393f23f1d61a7ed1fa3d764508ef1de9cd87ff
sourceRecordId=grammar20:We can read off the numbers shown on the dial. When we do so we are stating the 
originalSourceText=We can read off the numbers shown on the dial. When we do so we are stating the number of whole hours and minutes which have passed since noon or midnight. This option is always available.
In Russian we can state the number of minutes into the hour which is in progress. This makes sense up to the half hour.
We can say how many minutes remain until the little hand passes the next number on the dial. This is used after the half hour.
We can speak of quarters and halves of an hour rather than of minutes.
If the precise time is not important, then in Russian we can say things like “at the beginning of the hour” to indicate a time close to the start of the our or “at the end of the hour” for a time near its end.

The concept of the hour in progress will be unfamiliar to most English speakers. It is important to understand that “the first hour” does not mean one o'clock. It means the sixty-minute period which starts at 12 o'clock and ends at one o'clock. In the examples below the hour in progress is represented by a shaded pie slice.

The first hour began at 12 o'clock. To say that something happens at the time shown at left:
в двенадцать часов десять минут—at twelve hours ten minutes
в десять минут первого—at ten minutes of the first
в начале первого часа—at the beginning of the first hour

As the little hand passes the 1 the first hour ends and the second begins. To specify this time use any of these expressions:
в час пятнадцать минут—at [one] hour fifteen minutes
в пятнадцать минут второго—at the fifteenth minute of the second [hour]

в час двадцать пять минут—at [one] hour twenty five minutes
в двадцать пять минут второго—at twenty five minutes of the second [hour]

Once the hour is half-way done we may choose to say how soon the little hand will pass the next number. To specify the time shown at left:
в час тридцать пять минут—at [one] hour thirty five minutes
без двадцати пяти два—lacking twenty five minutes to the two
shippedText=at ten minutes of the first
transformation=readyrussian text extraction

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Numbers?oldid=4371358
sourceRevision=4371358
sourceRecordId=numbers:==Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal wit
originalSourceText===Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal with.  They all decline like adjectives.  For example: Только что фотографировала восемнадцатую девушку (She just photographed the eighteenth girl).

# п'''е'''рвый, п'''е'''рвая, п'''е'''рвое
# втор'''о'''й, втор'''а'''я, втор'''о'''е
# тр'''е'''тий, тр'''е'''тья, тр'''е'''тье
# четв'''ё'''ртый

### PROVENANCE `prov-u17-quarter-hour-rem`

- Objectives: obj-u17-quarter-hour
- Sessions: u17-s-orient, u17-s-quarter-hour
- Source: src-pressbooks-diverse-russian-traditions-grammar
- Rights: rights-pressbooks-diverse-russian-traditions-grammar
- Classification: none
- Current review status: IMPLEMENTED_PENDING_FINAL_AUDIT
- Content hash: f2de77d2c352f53da56058a7da58dad7ff70a281d10322b7448430291eb971be

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://wisc.pb.unizin.org/diverserussian/chapter/russia-traditions-grammar/
sourceRevision=ab2170e37d529a318f395f6e657d8024d0eaed8d7c5c9b34f8831d348b8b76b3
sourceRecordId=pressbooks-traditions-grammar:Грамматика: At what time on the quarter hour</strong></p>
</header>
<div class="
originalSourceText=Грамматика: At what time on the quarter hour</strong></p>
</header>
<div class="textbox__content">
<table class="grid" style="border-collapse: collapse;width: 100%">
<thead>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>Time</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%"><strong>Explanation and examples</strong></td>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the hour (0.00)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative, but note that the number then often requires genitive case after it. In informal speech, sometimes people leave out час / часа / часов. Remember: один час; два / три / четыре часа; пять часов.
<p>Концерт начинается <strong>в шесть часов</strong>. (18.00)<br>
Она была дома <strong>в час</strong>. (13.00)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the quarter hour (0.15)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + accusative with either <strong>четверть</strong> (quarter) or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the ordinal number of the next hour in the genitive case. In essence you are saying quarter of the next hour. Note that while you can say 6:15 as “шесть пятнадцать”, in the exercises on this page we would like to focus on this other way of expressing the time.
<p>Я приду <strong>в четверть восьмого</strong>. (7.15 – quarter of the eighth hour)<br>
Он нам позвонил <strong>в пятнадцать минут десятого</strong>. (9.15 – 15 minutes of the tenth hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the half hour (0.30)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>в</strong> + prepositional with <strong>половина</strong> and the genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour, or colloquially, <strong>пол_____</strong> (genitive case of the ordinal number of the next hour). Note that the spelling of 10:30 adds a hyphen: пол-одиннадцатого.
<p>Наш рейс уезжает <strong>в половине пятого</strong>. (4.30 – half of the fifth hour)<br>
Мы будем свободны <strong>в полвторого</strong>. (1.30 – half of the second hour)</p>
<p>If you need to review the ordinal numbers up to twelve, you can find them here:</p>
<div id="h5p-256">
<div class="h5p-iframe-wrapper"><iframe id="h5p-iframe-256" class="h5p-iframe" data-content-id="256" style="height:1px" src="about:blank" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="Ordinal numbers"></iframe></div>
</div>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="width: 33.3333%"><strong>At what time on the three quarters hour (0.45)</strong></td>
<td style="width: 66.6667%">Use <strong>без</strong> + genitive with <strong>четверть</strong> or <strong>пятнадцать минут</strong>. Then you use the cardinal number of the next hour in the nominative case. In essence you are saying X hours without X minutes.
<p>Передача начинается <strong>без четверти три</strong>. (2.45 – 3 without a quarter)<br>
Она обычно приходит на работу <strong>без пятнадцати девять</strong>. (8.45 – 9 without 15 minutes)</p>
shippedText=At what time on the hour (0.00)

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday

recordType=label
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday
